Thus we should implement a CUDA kernel that does: - Input q, k, cos, sin as described. - For each token and head, rotate the first `rot_dim` dimensions using the rotate_half formula. - The rest of the dimensions (from rot_dim to head_size) remain unchanged. So yes, only first `rot_dim` are modified. However, the C++ kernel also supports `rope_dim_offset`, but our contract doesn't have that, so it's 0. Thus our kernel should: - For each token t, for each head h: - Let base = q[t, h, :] (length head_size) - Let x = base[:rot_dim] (view as [embed_dim, 2]? Actually just 1D) - Compute rotate_half: split into first half (x0) and second half (x1) of size embed_dim each. - Compute x_rotated = cat([-x1, x0], dim=0) - Compute out_slice = x * cos[t] + x_rotated * sin[t] - Write out_slice back to base[:rot_dim] - Leave base[rot_dim:] unchanged. Since we are writing a custom CUDA kernel, we want it to be fast. This is a memory-bandwidth bound kernel: we read q, cos, sin, and write q. For each token and head, we read/write `rot_dim` elements of q, and `rot_dim` elements each of cos and sin (but cos and sin are shared across heads within a token). Actually cos and sin are per-token, shared across all heads. So cos/sin bandwidth is small compared to q/k. The kernel can be structured as: - Each thread block handles one or more tokens. - Within a block, threads process multiple heads and dimensions. To maximize throughput on H100: - Use 16-byte (float4 / half4 / __half2) vectorized loads and stores. Since dtype is half (fp16), we can use half4 (i.e., 8 bytes? Actually half is 2 bytes, so half4 is 8 bytes. For a thread block, if we process e.g., 1 token and all heads, we can cache cos and sin in shared memory: 2 * rot_dim * 2 bytes = 256 bytes for fp16, negligible. So we can load cos and sin into shared memory for the token(s) handled by the block. - Process multiple heads per block to increase parallelism and occupancy. Let's design the kernel for fp16. We assume q and k are fp16. cos and sin are fp16. Actually PyTorch's elementwise multiplication in fp16 on modern GPUs uses Tensor Cores or vector ALUs, but the result is rounded to fp16. So computing in fp32 and then converting to fp16 should match within tolerance. We can process multiple heads per thread to increase arithmetic intensity and reduce index calculations. Let's design the kernel: ``` template <typename T> __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( const T* __restrict__ q, const T* __restrict__ k, const T* __restrict__ cos, const T* __restrict__ sin, T* __restrict__ q_out, T* __restrict__ k_out, int num_tokens, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int head_size, int rot_dim, int q_stride_token, int q_stride_head, int k_stride_token, int k_stride_head, int cos_stride_token) { // blockIdx.x = token index const int t = blockIdx.x; if (t >= num_tokens) return; const int embed_dim = rot_dim / 2; // load cos and sin for this token into shared memory __shared__ T cos_shared[128]; // max rot_dim? we can allocate dynamic or fixed __shared__ T sin_shared[128]; // but rot_dim is known at runtime (64), so we can have fixed size or dynamic. // Since rot_dim <= head_size (128), we can allocate 128. ``` But shared memory size is small. Actually we can just load cos/sin directly from global memory, because they are coalesced and cached in L2. Since we have only 40 heads per token, each cos/sin element is read 40 times. It might be beneficial to put them in shared memory. But with 512 tokens, 40 heads, the L2 cache probably captures it well. However, shared memory is faster. Let's use shared memory for cos and sin. Since rot_dim=64, 128 halfs = 256 bytes. We can declare a fixed-size array of e.g., 256 halfs (max rot_dim 128? maybe head_size). To be safe, we can allocate 256 or use dynamic shared memory. On Hopper, shared memory capacity is 228KB per SM. We can easily allocate a few KB. Let's declare: ``` __shared__ alignas(16) half cos_smem[128]; __shared__ alignas(16) half sin_smem[128]; ``` Assume max rot_dim = 128. But we need to support generic `T` maybe fp16 or bf16. The problem says optimized for FP16. We can dispatch to `at::Half`. But we can write template. Actually torch.use `half` or `__half`. In PyTorch C++ extension, we can use `at::Half`. The kernel can be templated on scalar_t. Let's assume scalar_t is `at::Half` for this test. We can also support float/bfloat16 but the test uses float16. Memory access pattern: - q is stored as [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size]. So for token t, head h, the base pointer is `q + t * q_stride_token + h * q_stride_head`. The elements for the head are contiguous (since last dim is head_size). So we can load the first rot_dim elements as a contiguous block. - k similarly. - cos is [num_tokens, rot_dim], contiguous. - sin is [num_tokens, rot_dim], contiguous. For each token, we can have each thread process multiple head-dim pairs. One design: Each thread block handles one token. Block size = 256 threads. Total pairs per token: (num_heads + num_kv_heads) * embed_dim = (32+8)*32 = 1280 pairs. Wait, each head has embed_dim=32 pairs. So q has 32*32=1024 pairs, k has 8*32=256 pairs, total 1280 pairs. With 256 threads, each thread processes 5 pairs. That's a small number. We could instead have each thread process one pair for 5 heads, or something. Alternative: Each thread block handles multiple tokens. For example, blockDim.x = 256, and each block processes 4 tokens. Then total pairs per block = 4 * 1280 = 5120, ~20 pairs per thread. That might be better to amortize overhead. But the kernel is tiny; launch overhead might be significant relative to work. However, with 512 tokens, grid size of 512 or 128 is fine. On H100, we want enough thread blocks to fill all SMs. 512 blocks is plenty (H100 has ~144 SMs). So 512 blocks of 256 threads is fine. Let's do one token per block for simplicity and flexibility. 512 blocks. Within a block, we process all heads. Processing approach: - We can have a flat loop over all pairs across q and k: `total_pairs = (num_heads + num_kv_heads) * embed_dim`. Each thread handles `total_pairs / blockDim.x` pairs, with a loop. For a given pair index `p = threadIdx.x + i*blockDim.x`, we compute: `head_idx = p / embed_dim` `pair_idx = p % embed_dim` Determine if it's q or k head. Compute addresses. Load x,y,c0,s0,c1,s1. Compute and store. But this leads to scattered memory accesses for q/k. Specifically, different threads in a warp will access different heads, which may or may not be coalesced. If we store q as [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size], then for a fixed token, the memory layout is: head0: [0..head_size-1] head1: [head_stride..] If head_stride == head_size (contiguous in head dimension), then all heads are stored consecutively: [num_heads, head_size]. Then addressing is linear across the token. In that case, if we process pairs in order (head major or dim major), threads access adjacent memory. For q, shape [512,32,128] contiguous => stride(0)=4096, stride(1)=128, stride(2)=1. For a token, the offset of pair (h, i) (where i is dim in first half) is: `offset = t*4096 + h*128 + i` for x, and `+ embed_dim` for y. If we map thread index to linear index over (h, i), then adjacent threads will have adjacent `i` for same `h`, or adjacent `h` when `i` wraps. Since each head has 32 pairs (embed_dim), a warp of 32 threads will cover one full head's pairs. Then the x loads will be contiguous 32 halfs = 64 bytes. That's coalesced. The y loads will be at offset + embed_dim = +32, also contiguous 32 halfs. c0 loads from cos: all threads load cos[i] for same t but different i. cos is contiguous [512,64]. For token t, cos offset = t*64 + i. So contiguous 32 halfs = 64 bytes, coalesced. Similarly sin. c1 loads from cos[t, i+embed_dim]: offset = t*64 + i + 32, contiguous. Thus, mapping linear thread index to pairs in row-major order (h, i) gives fully coalesced loads/stores. Nice. Now, what about k? k shape [512,8,128] contiguous => stride(0)=1024, stride(1)=128, stride(2)=1. If we interleave q and k pairs in the linear index (e.g., first all q heads, then all k heads), then within a warp we might have both q and k accesses. But as long as the threads in a warp access the same token (which they do), and within the token they access contiguous memory, it's fine. However, q and k are separate tensors, so memory divergence per warp may happen if some threads access q and others access k. This is okay if they are separate loads, but it's better to process q and k in separate phases to avoid warp divergence and allow better scheduling. We can split the kernel into two loops: first process q, then process k. Better: Launch enough parallel work. Since 512 tokens * 40 heads * 32 pairs = 655,360 pairs. This is nice because memory access is fully coalesced for all loads/stores. We can assign each warp to a head and chunk, or use a flat grid. Since embed_dim=32, 16 __half2 operations per head. This is easy to implement with intrinsics. Even simpler: do it in FP32 but vectorized. Given that the kernel is small, I'll write a generic but efficient kernel: ``` template <typename scalar_t> __global__ void rotary_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ q, scalar_t* __restrict__ k, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin, int num_tokens, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int head_size, int rot_dim, int64_t q_stride_t, int64_t q_stride_h, int64_t k_stride_t, int64_t k_stride_h, int64_t cs_stride_t) { const int token = blockIdx.x; if (token >= num_tokens) return; const int embed_dim = rot_dim >> 1; // load cos/sin into shared memory extern __shared__ char smem[]; scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7); // pad to avoid bank conflict? // cooperative load of cos/sin for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < rot_dim; i += blockDim.x) { cos_smem[i] = cos[token * cs_stride_t + i]; sin_smem[i] = sin[token * cs_stride_t + i]; } __syncthreads(); // Process q const int total_q_pairs = num_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_q_pairs; idx += blockDim.x) { const int head = idx / embed_dim; const int pair = idx % embed_dim; scalar_t* q_ptr = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; const scalar_t x = q_ptr[pair]; const scalar_t y = q_ptr[pair + embed_dim]; const scalar_t c0 = cos_smem[pair]; const scalar_t s0 = sin_smem[pair]; const scalar_t c1 = cos_smem[pair + embed_dim]; const scalar_t s1 = sin_smem[pair + embed_dim]; float xf = static_cast<float>(x); float yf = static_cast<float>(y); float c0f = static_cast<float>(c0); float s0f = static_cast<float>(s0); float c1f = static_cast<float>(c1); float s1f = static_cast<float>(s1); q_ptr[pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(xf * c0f - yf * s0f); q_ptr[pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(yf * c1f + xf * s1f); } // Process k const int total_k_pairs = num_kv_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_k_pairs; idx += blockDim.x) { const int head = idx / embed_dim; const int pair = idx % embed_dim; scalar_t* k_ptr = k + token * k_stride_t + head * k_stride_h; const scalar_t x = k_ptr[pair]; const scalar_t y = k_ptr[pair + embed_dim]; const scalar_t c0 = cos_smem[pair]; const scalar_t s0 = sin_smem[pair]; const scalar_t c1 = cos_smem[pair + embed_dim]; const scalar_t s1 = sin_smem[pair + embed_dim]; float xf = static_cast<float>(x); float yf = static_cast<float>(y); float c0f = static_cast<float>(c0); float s0f = static_cast<float>(s0); float c1f = static_cast<float>(c1); float s1f = static_cast<float>(s1); k_ptr[pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(xf * c0f - yf * s0f); k_ptr[pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(yf * c1f + xf * s1f); } } ``` This kernel is simple and should be correct for the HF-style rotate_half with full cos/sin. But we need to ensure it matches the hidden reference exactly. Let's double-check: q_ptr[pair] corresponds to first half, q_ptr[pair+embed_dim] corresponds to second half. cos_smem[pair] = cos[pair]; cos_smem[pair+embed_dim] = cos[pair+embed_dim]. So we match the formula. Now, what about the vLLM kernel's behavior for `inverse`? The source had `inverse` boolean that negates sin. Our operation doesn't have inverse. We assume False. Another thing: The vLLM kernel has `rope_dim_offset`. Our operation starts at 0. What about `head_size`? We only modify first `rot_dim` dims; the rest remain unchanged from the input. Since q and k are in-place modified in the vLLM kernel, we should also modify in-place. Our kernel reads and writes to q and k in-place. That's fine since we only touch first rot_dim. Wait, the I/O contract: `forward(q, k, cos, sin)` returns outputs? The reference probably modifies q and k in-place or returns new tensors. In the `get_inputs`, they likely call `model(*inputs)` and compare outputs. We need to know if the hidden reference returns q, k or modifies them in-place. The vLLM kernel modifies in-place. The typical PyTorch module returns the modified tensors (or modifies in-place). The problem says "outputs match the hidden evaluation reference". We need to check if the reference returns anything. Since the source kernel modifies in-place, maybe the hidden reference also modifies in-place. But the `ModelNew.forward` should likely modify in-place and maybe return nothing or return q,k. We need to see what a typical `nn.Module` would do. The I/O contract says: `ModelNew must define __init__(*get_init_inputs()) and forward(*get_inputs()) so outputs match the hidden reference implementation on the evaluation GPU.` So `forward` gets `[q, k, cos, sin]`. It should return outputs that match the hidden reference. What does the hidden reference return? If it's a simple PyTorch module, it might return `(q, k)` after modifying them in-place. Or it might return a tuple. Let's assume it returns a tuple `(q_out, k_out)`. We should return `(q, k)` (modified in-place). Or to be safe, we can create copies? In-place modification is fine as long as the caller doesn't need the original. But PyTorch modules often don't modify inputs in-place unless specified. However, the vLLM kernel does modify in-place. The hidden reference could be a wrapper around that kernel, also in-place. If the evaluation does something like: ``` inputs = get_inputs() ref_out = ref_model(*inputs) inputs2 = get_inputs() new_out = model_new(*inputs2) # compare new_out and ref_out ``` Then in-place modification on `inputs2` is fine, as long as we return the modified tensors. But if `get_inputs()` returns the same tensors across calls (new calls generate new random tensors), it's fine. However, if the hidden reference modifies in-place and returns nothing (or returns the same tensors), the evaluation code might just compare the returned values. We should return `(q, k)`. Let's implement `forward` to modify q and k in-place and return `(q, k)`. Now, is there any possibility that the hidden reference uses the vLLM formula (single cos/sin per pair)? If we implement the HF formula with two cos/sin per pair, and the hidden reference uses one cos/sin per pair (ignoring second half), we will fail. To decide, let's think about what a "reference implementation" for a benchmark would be. Usually it's a simple but correct implementation in PyTorch. The HF `apply_rotary_pos_emb` is the standard reference. The vLLM kernel is an optimized version. The benchmark likely wants us to optimize the vLLM kernel. But the I/O changed slightly. Still, the core operation is the same: rotate q and k using cos and sin. The difference is whether cos and sin are duplicated. In the real vLLM, the cache is precomputed to avoid duplication. In the benchmark, they pass cos and sin separately because that's a natural interface for a module. But would the benchmark author give cos and sin of shape [NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM] where the second half is unused? It strikes me as unlikely. They would likely write a simple reference that uses the entire tensors, because writing code that slices off the second half is extra work and less natural. Therefore, the reference is most likely: ``` def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., : x.shape[-1] // 2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1] // 2 :] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) q_rot = q[:, :, :self.rot_dim] q_pass = q[:, :, self.rot_dim:] q_embed = (q_rot * cos.unsqueeze(1)) + (rotate_half(q_rot) * sin.unsqueeze(1)) q_out = torch.cat([q_embed, q_pass], dim=-1) # same for k return q_out, k_out ``` This is the most natural. It uses the full cos and sin. It matches the shapes exactly. It doesn't require any extra slicing or assumptions. Conclusion: Our kernel must implement exactly this formula. Now, we need to think about optimization for H100. The kernel above (one token per block, loops over pairs) is straightforward and will likely beat the PyTorch reference by a large margin because it avoids extra allocations and kernel launches (rotate_half involves cat and negation which launch separate kernels or at least extra memory traffic). But can we make it even faster? Yes, by vectorizing. Vectorization with half8 (16 bytes): We can load 8 consecutive halfs from the first half, 8 from the second half. That's 8 pairs per thread. The warp then loads 32*8 = 256 halfs = 512 bytes for x, and 512 bytes for y. This fully utilizes memory bandwidth. However, the computation is simple: x' = x*c0 - y*s0, y' = y*c1 + x*s1. We can do this in vectorized float or half. Let's implement a vectorized version using `__half2` and loops. Actually, to keep code generic and support rot_dim up to maybe 128, we can process in chunks of 8 halfs. We can define a helper: ``` struct alignas(16) Half8 { __half val[8]; }; ``` We can load/store with `reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(ptr)[0]`. But converting each element to float, computing, and converting back involves 8 operations. We can use `__half2` intrinsics to process two elements at a time, which may map to vector ALU instructions. But on Hopper, `__half` operations are native and fast. Converting to float might be slightly slower but still fine. Since it's memory-bound, the arithmetic cost is hidden. Let's implement a chunked loop: ``` const int pairs_per_iter = 8; // process 8 pairs per thread per iteration const int total_pairs_q = num_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x * pairs_per_iter; idx < total_pairs_q; idx += blockDim.x * pairs_per_iter) { int head = idx / embed_dim; int pair = idx % embed_dim; // if head or pair out of bounds, break scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; // load vectors // ... } ``` But this introduces divergence if `pairs_per_iter` doesn't evenly divide the head or block. For simplicity, we can process 4 pairs per thread (half4). Or we can use each thread to process a contiguous chunk of a head. Actually, since a head has 32 pairs, and block size is 256, we have 256/32 = 8 threads per head if we assign contiguous threads to a head. But mapping threads to heads can be done via thread blocks/warps. Simpler design: One warp (32 threads) processes one head. - Warp 0..7 process q heads 0..7. - Warp 8..15 process q heads 8..15. - ... - Warp 31 processes q head 31 and k head 0..7? No. Wait, 32 q heads means 32 warps. Plus 8 k heads = 8 warps. Total 40 warps. A block has at most 32 warps (1024 threads). So we need at least 2 blocks per token if we assign one warp per head. But we can use 1 block of 1024 threads (32 warps) to process 32 q heads. Then a second block or second kernel for k. Or we can have block size 512 (16 warps) and process 16 heads per block, needing 2 blocks for q and 1 for k. This is getting complicated. Back to the simple 256-thread block with loop. Let's compute performance. - 512 tokens, 256 threads each => 131072 threads. - Each thread processes total_pairs_per_token / 256 = 1280/256 = 5 pairs on average. - Each pair: 2 loads + 4 smem loads + 2 stores + some arithmetic. Total memory per pair: 4 bytes (x,y in, c0,s0,c1,s1 in from smem, x,y out). Actually q/k memory traffic dominates. Per pair: read x (2B), read y (2B), write x (2B), write y (2B) = 8B of global memory. Plus cos/sin from smem (negligible). Per thread: 5 pairs * 8B = 40B of global memory traffic. 131072 threads * 40B = ~5.2MB, matching data size. At 3.35 TB/s, 5.2MB takes 1.55 us. Kernel launch overhead can be ~3-5 us. Total maybe ~5-7 us. The PyTorch reference uses multiple ops: 1. Slice q[..., :rot_dim] -> view, no copy. 2. rotate_half: involves slicing, negation, cat. This likely launches 2-3 kernels or at least does extra memory passes. 3. Elementwise mul and add. Total memory traffic maybe 3-4x the data size, taking ~10-20 us. So our simple kernel should give a nice speedup. To squeeze more performance, we can unroll the loop, use `float2` or vector loads, and load cos/sin into registers or smem. But given the problem size, vectorizing might help only marginally. However, the competition might expect near-SOL bandwidth. So vectorization is good. Let's do vectorized loads of 8 halfs (16 bytes) per thread. For warp-level coalescing: - Thread 0 loads pairs 0..7 from head 0. - Thread 1 loads pairs 8..15 from head 0. ... - Thread 3 loads pairs 24..31 from head 0. - Thread 4 loads pairs 0..7 from head 1. ... Since a warp has 32 threads, it can handle 8 heads (32/4 = 8) fully per iteration. But our loop doesn't need to be per warp. We can just assign a linear index to each thread, but ensure we align to 8. Better: Use thread index to compute a global chunk index. ``` const int pairs_per_chunk = 8; const int num_chunks_per_head = embed_dim / pairs_per_chunk; // 4 const int total_q_chunks = num_heads * num_chunks_per_head; // 128 for (int chunk = threadIdx.x; chunk < total_q_chunks; chunk += blockDim.x) { int head = chunk / num_chunks_per_head; int chunk_in_head = chunk % num_chunks_per_head; int pair = chunk_in_head * pairs_per_chunk; scalar_t* base = q + token*q_stride_t + head*q_stride_h; // load x_vec from base + pair // load y_vec from base + pair + embed_dim // load c0_vec, s0_vec, c1_vec, s1_vec from smem // compute elementwise // store } ``` This is clean. `total_q_chunks` = 128. `blockDim.x` = 256. So half the threads process one chunk each, the other half are idle for q. Then for k, `total_k_chunks` = 8*4 = 32. Threads 0..31 process them. So many threads are idle. This is okay but not ideal. We can reduce blockDim.x to 128. Then for q: 128 chunks, exactly 1 per thread. For k: 32 chunks, 1/4 of threads. Block of 128 threads => 4 warps. Occupancy is great. Grid of 512 blocks. Total threads = 65536. Each thread does about 8 pairs (128 halfs? no, 8 pairs = 16 halfs). Actually each chunk is 8 pairs = 16 halfs. Total q work: 128 chunks * 16 halfs = 2048 halfs per token. Wait, 32 heads * 64 dims = 2048 halfs. Yes. So 128 threads each handle 16 halfs of q, and then some handle k. This is good. But with blockDim.x = 128, we have fewer threads per block, but more blocks can run concurrently per SM. On H100, up to 2048 threads per SM. With 128 threads per block, we can have 16 blocks per SM. With 512 blocks total, occupancy is high. However, 128 threads per block might not saturate memory bandwidth if each block accesses only one token (5KB). Memory transactions per block are small. But with many blocks concurrently, the memory controller can interleave them. H100's memory subsystem is powerful. Alternatively, blockDim.x = 256 is fine too. Let's stick with 256 threads for simplicity, but chunk processing. Even if half threads are idle for q or k, it's okay because the work is tiny and the GPU will run through it quickly. Actually we can make blockDim.x = 128. Let's decide: blockDim.x = 256. Process q and k in a single loop with a combined total chunk count. ``` int total_q_chunks = num_heads * (embed_dim / pairs_per_chunk); int total_k_chunks = num_kv_heads * (embed_dim / pairs_per_chunk); int total_chunks = total_q_chunks + total_k_chunks; for (int chunk = threadIdx.x; chunk < total_chunks; chunk += blockDim.x) { bool is_q = chunk < total_q_chunks; int local_chunk = is_q ? chunk : chunk - total_q_chunks; int heads = is_q ? num_heads : num_kv_heads; int head = local_chunk / num_chunks_per_head; int chunk_in_head = local_chunk % num_chunks_per_head; int pair = chunk_in_head * pairs_per_chunk; scalar_t* ptr = is_q ? (q + token*q_stride_t + head*q_stride_h) : (k + token*k_stride_t + head*k_stride_h); // load x_vec, y_vec // load c0,s0,c1,s1 from smem // compute and store } ``` This mixes q and k in the same loop. Since q and k are separate tensors, a warp might have some threads doing q and some doing k, causing divergence because ptr differs. Divergence means two execution paths. But since the operations are identical (same instructions, different base pointer), warp divergence is not too bad. However, memory coalescing might be affected if threads access q and k simultaneously in a warp. Since q and k are separate allocations, they cannot be coalesced together. A warp with mixed q/k accesses will issue two separate load/store instructions. That's okay. To avoid divergence, we can process q first, then k. ``` // q for (int chunk = threadIdx.x; chunk < total_q_chunks; chunk += blockDim.x) { ... } __syncthreads(); // maybe not needed // k for (int chunk = threadIdx.x; chunk < total_k_chunks; chunk += blockDim.x) { ... } ``` With blockDim.x = 256, total_q_chunks = 128. So threads 0..127 process one chunk each, threads 128..255 idle. Then total_k_chunks = 32. Threads 0..31 process one chunk each, others idle. This is simple and divergence-free. Now, the vector struct. We can define: ``` template <typename T> struct Vec8 { T data[8]; }; ``` But we need to load and store aligned. Since head_size is 128, and chunk size is 8 pairs = 16 halfs = 32 bytes, alignment is fine if pair is multiple of 8. pair = chunk_in_head * 8. So yes, aligned to 16 bytes? 8*(sizeof(half)) = 16 bytes. For chunk_in_head=0, pair=0, aligned. For chunk_in_head=1, pair=8, offset 16 bytes, aligned to 16. Since head_stride is 128 halfs = 256 bytes, also aligned. Now, dispatch in Python: ModelNew: ``` class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): super().__init__() self.rot_dim = rot_dim def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): # q: [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size] # k: [num_tokens, num_kv_heads, head_size] # cos, sin: [num_tokens, rot_dim] num_tokens = q.size(0) num_heads = q.size(1) head_size = q.size(2) num_kv_heads = k.size(1) # launch kernel # using torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline ``` But we should compile the inline extension inside `__init__` and cache it, or compile once in the file. Since `load_inline` compiles on first call, we can do it at module initialization. But we need to include the C++ code as a string. Wait, the environment might not allow writing files. `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` compiles a temporary .cu file. This is standard. We need to be careful about compilation time. The code should be concise. Now, the kernel launch configuration: ``` int embed_dim = rot_dim / 2; dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(256); size_t smem_size = 2 * ((rot_dim + 7) / 8) * 8 * sizeof(at::Half); // pad to 8 halfs // Actually we can compute: cos_smem needs rot_dim elements, sin_smem needs rot_dim elements, plus padding. size_t smem_size = 2 * ((rot_dim * sizeof(at::Half) + 15) / 16) * 16; rotary_embedding_kernel<<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>(...); ``` Wait, in the kernel we declared: ``` scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7); ``` This assumes `cos_smem` and `sin_smem` are aligned to 16 bytes? `smem` is aligned to 16 bytes by default. `cos_smem` starts at 0. `sin_smem` offset in elements = `((rot_dim + 7) & ~7)`. In bytes: offset * sizeof(scalar_t) = `((rot_dim + 7) & ~7) * 2`. For rot_dim=64, offset=64 halfs = 128 bytes. But `((64+7)&~7) = 64`. 64*2=128. Next multiple of 16 after 128 is 128 itself. Good. For rot_dim=66, offset=72 halfs = 144 bytes, which is multiple of 16. Good. So this works. Dynamic shared memory size: we need space for `2 * ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7)` elements. `size_t smem_bytes = 2 * ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7) * sizeof(scalar_t);` Now, we need to handle the case where `q` or `k` might be non-contiguous. In the test they are contiguous. To be safe, we can pass strides. For q: `q_stride_t = q.stride(0)`, `q_stride_h = q.stride(1)`. For k: `k_stride_t = k.stride(0)`, `k_stride_h = k.stride(1)`. For cos: `cs_stride_t = cos.stride(0)`. Since cos is 2D contiguous, stride is rot_dim. We need to launch from Python using custom op. But since it's inline C++, we can call the kernel directly in the C++ function that receives the tensors. We should write a C++ function `rotary_embedding_forward` that takes tensors and launches the kernel. Then call it from Python via the loaded extension. C++ code: ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> using scalar_t = at::Half; __device__ inline __half2 load_h2(const scalar_t* p) { return *reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(p); } __device__ inline void store_h2(scalar_t* p, __half2 v) { *reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(p) = v; } __global__ void rotary_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ q, scalar_t* __restrict__ k, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin, int num_tokens, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int head_size, int rot_dim, int64_t q_stride_t, int64_t q_stride_h, int64_t k_stride_t, int64_t k_stride_h, int64_t cs_stride_t) { const int token = blockIdx.x; if (token >= num_tokens) return; const int embed_dim = rot_dim >> 1; extern __shared__ char smem[]; scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7); const scalar_t* cos_g = cos + token * cs_stride_t; const scalar_t* sin_g = sin + token * cs_stride_t; for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < rot_dim; i += blockDim.x) { cos_smem[i] = cos_g[i]; sin_smem[i] = sin_g[i]; } __syncthreads(); const int half2_per_head = embed_dim >> 1; const int total_q = num_heads * half2_per_head; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_q; idx += blockDim.x) { const int head = idx / half2_per_head; const int pair2 = idx % half2_per_head; const int pair = pair2 << 1; scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; __half2 xv = load_h2(base + pair); __half2 yv = load_h2(base + pair + embed_dim); __half2 c0 = load_h2(cos_smem + pair); __half2 s0 = load_h2(sin_smem + pair); __half2 c1 = load_h2(cos_smem + pair + embed_dim); __half2 s1 = load_h2(sin_smem + pair + embed_dim); float2 xf = __half22float2(xv); float2 yf = __half22float2(yv); float2 c0f = __half22float2(c0); float2 s0f = __half22float2(s0); float2 c1f = __half22float2(c1); float2 s1f = __half22float2(s1); float2 out_xf, out_yf; out_xf.x = xf.x * c0f.x - yf.x * s0f.x; out_xf.y = xf.y * c0f.y - yf.y * s0f.y; out_yf.x = yf.x * c1f.x + xf.x * s1f.x; out_yf.y = yf.y * c1f.y + xf.y * s1f.y; store_h2(base + pair, __float22half2_rn(out_xf)); store_h2(base + pair + embed_dim, __float22half2_rn(out_yf)); } const int total_k = num_kv_heads * half2_per_head; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_k; idx += blockDim.x) { const int head = idx / half2_per_head; const int pair2 = idx % half2_per_head; const int pair = pair2 << 1; scalar_t* base = k + token * k_stride_t + head * k_stride_h; __half2 xv = load_h2(base + pair); __half2 yv = load_h2(base + pair + embed_dim); __half2 c0 = load_h2(cos_smem + pair); __half2 s0 = load_h2(sin_smem + pair); __half2 c1 = load_h2(cos_smem + pair + embed_dim); __half2 s1 = load_h2(sin_smem + pair + embed_dim); float2 xf = __half22float2(xv); float2 yf = __half22float2(yv); float2 c0f = __half22float2(c0); float2 s0f = __half22float2(s0); float2 c1f = __half22float2(c1); float2 s1f = __half22float2(s1); float2 out_xf, out_yf; out_xf.x = xf.x * c0f.x - yf.x * s0f.x; out_xf.y = xf.y * c0f.y - yf.y * s0f.y; out_yf.x = yf.x * c1f.x + xf.x * s1f.x; out_yf.y = yf.y * c1f.y + xf.y * s1f.y; store_h2(base + pair, __float22half2_rn(out_xf)); store_h2(base + pair + embed_dim, __float22half2_rn(out_yf)); } // handle scalar tail if embed_dim is odd if (embed_dim & 1) { // This means embed_dim % 2 != 0. half2_per_head = embed_dim/2 truncated. // Need to process the last pair (index embed_dim-1) for each head. const int tail_pair = embed_dim - 1; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < num_heads; idx += blockDim.x) { scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + idx * q_stride_h; scalar_t xv = base[tail_pair]; scalar_t yv = base[tail_pair + embed_dim]; scalar_t c0 = cos_smem[tail_pair]; scalar_t s0 = sin_smem[tail_pair]; scalar_t c1 = cos_smem[tail_pair + embed_dim]; scalar_t s1 = sin_smem[tail_pair + embed_dim]; float xf = static_cast<float>(xv); float yf = static_cast<float>(yv); float c0f = static_cast<float>(c0); float s0f = static_cast<float>(s0); float c1f = static_cast<float>(c1); float s1f = static_cast<float>(s1); base[tail_pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(xf * c0f - yf * s0f); base[tail_pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(yf * c1f + xf * s1f); } for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < num_kv_heads; idx += blockDim.x) { scalar_t* base = k + token * k_stride_t + idx * k_stride_h; scalar_t xv = base[tail_pair]; scalar_t yv = base[tail_pair + embed_dim]; scalar_t c0 = cos_smem[tail_pair]; scalar_t s0 = sin_smem[tail_pair]; scalar_t c1 = cos_smem[tail_pair + embed_dim]; scalar_t s1 = sin_smem[tail_pair + embed_dim]; float xf = static_cast<float>(xv); float yf = static_cast<float>(yv); float c0f = static_cast<float>(c0); float s0f = static_cast<float>(s0); float c1f = static_cast<float>(c1); float s1f = static_cast<float>(s1); base[tail_pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(xf * c0f - yf * s0f); base[tail_pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(yf * c1f + xf * s1f); } } } void rotary_embedding_forward( torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin, int rot_dim) { int num_tokens = q.size(0); int num_heads = q.size(1); int head_size = q.size(2); int num_kv_heads = k.size(1); int64_t q_stride_t = q.stride(0); int64_t q_stride_h = q.stride(1); int64_t k_stride_t = k.stride(0); int64_t k_stride_h = k.stride(1); int64_t cs_stride_t = cos.stride(0); dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(256); size_t smem_size = 2 * ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7) * sizeof(scalar_t); const at::cuda::OptionalCUDAGuard device_guard(q.device()); const cudaStream_t stream = at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); rotary_kernel<<<grid, block, smem_size, stream>>>( q.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), k.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), cos.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), sin.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, rot_dim, q_stride_t, q_stride_h, k_stride_t, k_stride_h, cs_stride_t); } ``` Then Python side: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): super().__init__() self.rot_dim = rot_dim self._cached = False self._op = None def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): if not self._cached: import torch.utils.cpp_extension as ext cuda_source = """ ... C++ code ... """ self._op = ext.load_inline( name="rotary_embedding_hopper", cpp_sources="""... torch binding ...""", cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["rotary_embedding_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], verbose=False, ) self._cached = True self._op.rotary_embedding_forward(q, k, cos, sin, self.rot_dim) return q, k ``` But `load_inline` can be called only once. We should compile it outside or in `__init__`. Actually, compiling in `forward` on first call is okay but slightly risky because the first forward will be slow. However, evaluation likely does a warm-up or measures after warm-up. To be safe, we can compile in `__init__`. But `load_inline` might fail if the CUDA context is not initialized yet? Usually it's fine. We can just compile in `__init__` and store the module. One nuance: `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` may need `cpp_sources` and `cuda_sources`. The `cpp_sources` should declare the function. The `cuda_sources` contains the actual kernel and a wrapper. Simpler: We can put everything in `cuda_sources` and use `with_cuda=True`. Or we can provide both. Example: ```python cpp_source = """ void rotary_embedding_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin, int rot_dim); """ cuda_source = """ #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> ... void rotary_embedding_forward(...) { ... } """ ``` Then `ext.load_inline(name="rotary", cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["rotary_embedding_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], verbose=False)`. We need to make sure the extension is compiled only once. We can do it as a module-level variable or inside `__init__`. Let's write the whole code. Wait, there is a potential issue: The kernel modifies q and k in-place. If the Tensors are not contiguous in memory or have weird strides, the kernel should still work because we use strides. But our vector loads assume alignment to 4 bytes. If a tensor is not contiguous but still has even strides, it's fine. If head_stride is odd, our vector loads will crash with misaligned address. To be robust, we could check alignment and fall back to scalar loads. But for the benchmark, inputs are contiguous. Let's add a simple check or just use the kernel. Alternatively, we can always process in scalar mode to be 100% safe. Performance difference is small for this workload. Let's calculate scalar kernel performance. Scalar kernel with block=256, grid=512. Each thread processes ~5 pairs. Each pair: 2 global loads (x,y), 2 global stores, 4 smem loads. With coalescing, memory throughput is good. The arithmetic is negligible. The memory bandwidth is the bottleneck. Since the bus is 3.35 TB/s, and data is ~5MB, we need ~1.5us. Scalar kernel should achieve close to that because the memory controller handles coalescing of 2-byte accesses fine, though each warp issues more instructions. However, the compiler might not generate optimal scalar memory instructions. But it's still fine. Actually, to be safe with alignment and keep the kernel simpler, maybe we should do a scalar kernel but process 2 pairs per thread in a small unrolled loop to help the compiler. Given that we have limited time for compilation and correctness checks, simpler is better. Let's go with the scalar kernel but good parallelism. Wait, I want to get as much speedup as possible. Let's think about the PyTorch reference. The reference does: ``` q_rot = q[..., :rot_dim] q_pass = q[..., rot_dim:] q_rot = q_rot * cos.unsqueeze(1) + rotate_half(q_rot) * sin.unsqueeze(1) q_out = torch.cat([q_rot, q_pass], dim=-1) ``` How many kernel launches does this involve? 1. `q_rot = q[..., :rot_dim]` -> no kernel, just view. 2. `rotate_half(q_rot)`: This is `torch.cat((-q_rot[..., rot_dim/2:], q_rot[..., :rot_dim/2]), dim=-1)`. This involves a slice, negation, and cat. In PyTorch, `cat` on the last dim of contiguous tensors might be fused or not? Actually, the elementwise expression `q_rot * cos + rotate_half(q_rot) * sin` might be fused by the JIT or not. In eager mode, it will be multiple kernels: - Kernel for negation of second half. - Kernel for cat (or maybe cat is a view if contiguous? But here it's not in-place, so cat copies). - Kernel for q_rot * cos. - Kernel for rotated * sin. - Kernel for add. Total maybe 5 kernels, each reading/writing memory. So total memory traffic could be 5-6x the data size. Our custom kernel does it in one pass. So speedup should be ~5x. Given that, even a scalar kernel is fine. But let's try to vectorize because it's not too hard and we already wrote the logic. I'll write the vectorized kernel with `__half2`, but also include a scalar tail for safety. One more detail: Hopper supports warp group clusters, TMA, etc., but for this tiny kernel it's overkill. We just need to use the right architecture flags (`-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90`) and good block size. Let's finalize the code. Wait, there is a nuance about `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline`: In some environments, it requires `extra_include_paths` or `verbose=True` to debug. But we want it to compile silently. We need to include `<torch/extension.h>` and `#include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h>`. Also, we should ensure the function is declared in `cpp_sources` so that PyTorch binding can find it. Let's write the full Python class. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn NUM_TOKENS = 512 NUM_HEADS = 32 NUM_KV_HEADS = 8 HEAD_SIZE = 128 ROT_DIM = 64 def get_inputs(): q = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, NUM_HEADS, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() k = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, NUM_KV_HEADS, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() cos = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() sin = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() return [q, k, cos, sin] def get_init_inputs(): return [ROT_DIM] class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): super().__init__() self.rot_dim = rot_dim import torch.utils.cpp_extension as ext cpp_source = "void rotary_embedding_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin, int rot_dim);" cuda_source = """ #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> using scalar_t = at::Half; if (token >= num_tokens) return; const int embed_dim = rot_dim >> 1; extern __shared__ char smem[]; scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7); } """ self.module = ext.load_inline( name="rotary_embedding_hopper", cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["rotary_embedding_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): self.module.rotary_embedding_forward(q, k, cos, sin, self.rot_dim) return q, k ``` This is a full self-contained model. But wait: `torch.utils.cpp_extension.load_inline` may need `with_cuda=True` or it detects it. Usually yes. But if `cpp_sources` is provided, and `cuda_sources` is provided, it knows. We can set `with_cuda=True`. Now, regarding the C++ wrapper: The function `rotary_embedding_forward` is declared in `cpp_sources` and defined in `cuda_sources`. That should work. Simple scalar kernel: ``` __global__ void rotary_kernel(...) { int token = blockIdx.x; if (token >= num_tokens) return; int embed_dim = rot_dim >> 1; extern __shared__ char smem[]; scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((rot_dim + 7) & ~7); const scalar_t* cos_g = cos + token * cs_stride_t; const scalar_t* sin_g = sin + token * cs_stride_t; for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < rot_dim; i += blockDim.x) { cos_smem[i] = cos_g[i]; sin_smem[i] = sin_g[i]; } __syncthreads(); int total_q = num_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_q; idx += blockDim.x) { int head = idx / embed_dim; int pair = idx % embed_dim; scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; float x = static_cast<float>(base[pair]); float y = static_cast<float>(base[pair + embed_dim]); float c0 = static_cast<float>(cos_smem[pair]); float s0 = static_cast<float>(sin_smem[pair]); float c1 = static_cast<float>(cos_smem[pair + embed_dim]); float s1 = static_cast<float>(sin_smem[pair + embed_dim]); base[pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x * c0 - y * s0); base[pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y * c1 + x * s1); } int total_k = num_kv_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_k; idx += blockDim.x) { int head = idx / embed_dim; int pair = idx % embed_dim; scalar_t* base = k + token * k_stride_t + head * k_stride_h; float x = static_cast<float>(base[pair]); float y = static_cast<float>(base[pair + embed_dim]); float c0 = static_cast<float>(cos_smem[pair]); float s0 = static_cast<float>(sin_smem[pair]); float c1 = static_cast<float>(cos_smem[pair + embed_dim]); float s1 = static_cast<float>(sin_smem[pair + embed_dim]); base[pair] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x * c0 - y * s0); base[pair + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y * c1 + x * s1); } } ``` This is extremely clean and safe. It matches the HF formula exactly. It has no alignment issues. It uses shared memory for cos/sin. It uses fp32 arithmetic. Block size: 256. Grid: num_tokens. Some models use partial rotary embedding, where only the first `rot_dim` dims are rotated. In Llama-3, maybe? Or in some implementations, `rotary_dim = head_size // 2`? Actually, some models like GPT-NeoX use partial rotation. But Llama uses full rotation. Wait, Llama uses `head_dim` for rotary. Yes, in `LlamaRotaryEmbedding`, `self.dim = self.head_size`. So `rot_dim = head_size`. But the test has `rot_dim=64` and `head_size=128`. That suggests partial rotation. In vLLM, there is `rot_dim` and `rope_dim_offset`. They support partial rotation. Okay, final kernel formula: - For each pair `i` in `0..embed_dim-1`: - `x = base[i]` - `y = base[i + embed_dim]` - `c = cos_smem[i]` - `s = sin_smem[i]` - `base[i] = x*c - y*s` - `base[i + embed_dim] = y*c + x*s` This uses only the first half of cos and sin. Now, I need to produce the final code. One more optimization idea: Since we only use the first half of cos and sin, we only need to load `embed_dim` elements each into smem, not `rot_dim`. That halves smem usage. Actually, we can load only the first `embed_dim` of cos and sin (since the second half is ignored). But if the hidden reference uses full arrays (HF style), we'd be wrong. Since we decided on vLLM style, we load only first half. But to be safe if the hidden reference actually uses full arrays... No, we must commit. Loading only first half: ``` for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < embed_dim; i += blockDim.x) { cos_smem[i] = cos_g[i]; sin_smem[i] = sin_g[i]; } ``` Smem size = 2 * ((embed_dim + 7) & ~7) * sizeof(scalar_t). This saves a bit of shared memory and global loads. Not critical, but nice. However, what about alignment for `__half2` loads from smem? If we only store embed_dim=32 elements, and we load up to pair=30, we are within bounds. We don't need padding for the second half. But we still want `sin_smem` aligned to 16 bytes. So offset = `((embed_dim + 7) & ~7) * sizeof(scalar_t)`. For embed_dim=32, offset=64 bytes. Not multiple of 16? 64 is multiple of 16. For embed_dim=33, offset=72*2=144 bytes, multiple of 16. Good. With 512 threads, each thread handles exactly 2 pairs for q. For k, total pairs = 256, so half the threads handle 1 pair, half idle. 512 threads is not bad. In our kernel, if we use 512 threads: - total_q = 1024. Each thread does 2 pairs (loop with step 512, 2 iterations). - total_k = 256. Per SM 65536 registers => 5 blocks per SM. That fills 2560 threads, close to the max 2048. Actually 5*512=2560 > 2048, so occupancy limited to 4 blocks per SM (2048 threads). Good. Let's use 512 threads. Grid = num_tokens = 512. Total blocks = 512. SMs = 144. So blocks per SM = ~3.5. Good occupancy. Alternatively, 256 threads => 8 blocks per SM, 2048 threads. """ cuda_source = """ #include <torch/extension.h> #include <ATen/cuda/CUDAContext.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> using scalar_t = at::Half; __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ q, scalar_t* __restrict__ k, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin, int num_tokens, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int head_size, int rot_dim, int64_t q_stride_t, int64_t q_stride_h, int64_t k_stride_t, int64_t k_stride_h, int64_t cs_stride_t) { const int token = blockIdx.x; if (token >= num_tokens) return; const int embed_dim = rot_dim >> 1; extern __shared__ char smem[]; scalar_t* cos_smem = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem); scalar_t* sin_smem = cos_smem + ((embed_dim + 7) & ~7); const float x = static_cast<float>(base[pair]); const float y = static_cast<float>(base[pair + embed_dim]); const float c = static_cast<float>(cos_smem[pair]); const float s = static_cast<float>(sin_smem[pair]); For memory bandwidth, each thread reads: - 2 fp16 from q (x and y) - 2 fp16 from k (x and y, if processing both) - 2 fp16 from cos/sin (shared memory, so doesn't count toward global bandwidth) - Writes 2 fp16 to q - Writes 2 fp16 to k Total global memory traffic per pair: 8 bytes read + 8 bytes write = 16 bytes. Therefore, our kernel needs to read cos and sin of shape [num_tokens, rot_dim] and use different values for the first and second halves. We need to change the indexing accordingly. Also, block size: the workload per token is `num_heads * rot_dim` for q and `num_kv_heads * rot_dim` for k. Since rot_dim = 64, total elements per token for q is 32 * 64 = 2048, for k is 8 * 64 = 512. Total per token = 2560 float16s. With 512 tokens, total = ~1.3M f16 elements. Memory bandwidth bound. For H100, we should maximize parallelism and coalescing. Since each token is independent, using one block per token with many threads is good. But we can also consider one block processing multiple tokens to reduce block overhead, or one warp per head. Let's think about occupancy and memory throughput. Given H100 has huge memory bandwidth (3.35 TB/s), the kernel is memory bandwidth bound. The number of blocks = 512. With 132 SMs, that's about 4 blocks per SM. If we use 256 threads per block, we get 256 * 512 = 131072 threads. H100 can handle many. But we want to maximize memory coalescing: each thread should load/store contiguous elements. Since each head's rot_dim (64) elements are contiguous in memory. Let's organize threads to process contiguous elements. One approach: for each token block, process all heads. We can have each thread handle one pair (i.e., two elements: x and y). Since each pair is at offset i and i+embed_dim, they are not contiguous across the boundary, but within a warp, if threads process consecutive i's, then loads of x are coalesced, loads of y are coalesced (offset by embed_dim), loads of cos are coalesced, loads of sin are coalesced. This is good. Total pairs per token for q: num_heads * embed_dim = 32 * 32 = 1024. For k: 8 * 32 = 256. Total per token = 1280 pairs. 1280 pairs * 2 elem/pair = 2560 elements. If block size = 256 threads, and we have 1 block per token: - Each thread can process ~5 pairs (1280/256 = 5). This is good. - But the loops over heads and embed_dim can be combined into a single loop over total pairs. But we also need to handle that q and k have different shapes. We can process q first, then k. Alternative: use 1 warp (32 threads) per head. 32 warps = 1024 threads per block. Then each warp handles one head's 32 pairs, each thread handling one pair. For q (32 heads), we need 32 warps. For k (8 heads), 8 warps. But block size is fixed. If we use 1024 threads, we can handle 32 heads per token. But then for k, only 8 warps do work, the rest are idle. This is fine if k is smaller. But we want to ensure good occupancy. 1024 threads per block * 512 blocks = 524288 threads. Register limit: 255 registers per thread max, but if we use many registers, occupancy drops. Each thread reads/writes two elements, no shared memory needed if we load cos/sin directly from global memory. Since the cos/sin for each token are reused across all heads, caching them in shared memory or L1 could help. On H100, L1 cache is large (up to 228KB shared/L1 partition). So maybe shared memory isn't necessary. But cos/sin are only 64 f16 elements = 128 bytes per token! That's tiny and will stay in L1 automatically. So we don't need explicit shared memory. Thus, a simple global memory kernel with 1 thread per pair is sufficient. We should try to maximize warp execution efficiency. Let's do: grid = num_tokens block = 256 (or 128 or 512) Each thread handles multiple pairs: ```c++ int total_pairs_q = num_heads * embed_dim; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_pairs_q; idx += blockDim.x) { int head = idx / embed_dim; int pair = idx % embed_dim; scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; float x = base[pair]; float y = base[pair + embed_dim]; float c1 = cos[token * cs_stride_t + pair]; float s1 = sin[token * cs_stride_t + pair]; float c2 = cos[token * cs_stride_t + pair + embed_dim]; float s2 = sin[token * cs_stride_t + pair + embed_dim]; base[pair] = x * c1 - y * s1; base[pair + embed_dim] = y * c2 + x * s2; } ``` Similarly for k. Will this match the hidden reference? Let's test with a quick CPU-like implementation: ```python import torch q = torch.randn(2, 2, 8) k = torch.randn(2, 1, 8) cos = torch.randn(2, 8) sin = torch.randn(2, 8) def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., :x.shape[-1]//2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1]//2:] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) def ref(q, k, cos, sin): c = cos.unsqueeze(1) s = sin.unsqueeze(1) q_rot = q[..., :8] * c + rotate_half(q[..., :8]) * s k_rot = k[..., :8] * c + rotate_half(k[..., :8]) * s q_out = q.clone() k_out = k.clone() q_out[..., :8] = q_rot k_out[..., :8] = k_rot return q_out, k_out ``` Let's compute by hand for one element: x1 = q[..., 0], x2 = q[..., 4]. rotate_half => -x2 at pos 0. So q_rot[..., 0] = x1 * cos[..., 0] + (-x2) * sin[..., 0]. That matches `x * c1 - y * s1`. q_rot[..., 4] = x2 * cos[..., 4] + x1 * sin[..., 4]. That matches `y * c2 + x * s2`. Yes! So our kernel logic should be exactly that. Now, what about `q_stride_t` and `q_stride_h`? In the input `q` is [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size], so `q.stride(0) = num_heads * head_size`, `q.stride(1) = head_size`. Thus token_head offset = token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h. If so, in-place modification of the input tensor still yields correct returned values (the input tensor in the caller will also be modified, but the caller may not use it afterward). Usually, the evaluation harness calls `model.forward(*inputs)` and compares outputs; it doesn't reuse the inputs. So in-place is fine. Now, about performance: For H100, we should try to use vectorized loads/stores (`half2`) to improve memory throughput. Since the pairs are at offset `pair` and `pair + embed_dim`, which are separated by 32 elements (64 bytes), a single thread handles both. We cannot easily load them as a single vector because they are not contiguous. But we can load `x` and `y` as `__half` (16-bit). H100 has excellent 16-bit throughput. The overhead of scalar loads is okay. However, we can vectorize the cos/sin loads since for consecutive pairs (pair, pair+1), we can load `half2`. Similarly for q and k? `base[pair]` is contiguous for consecutive pairs within the first half. So a thread could process two pairs at once: load `half2 x2 = * (half2*)(base + pair);` and `half2 y2 = * (half2*)(base + pair + embed_dim);`. But then we need cos and sin for both pairs: `half2 c1 = *(half2*)(cos + ...);` etc. This halves the number of memory instructions and improves throughput. But for simplicity and correctness, let's first get a scalar kernel working. Then we can try to vectorize. Another optimization: use `__restrict__` and `__half` arithmetic directly. In eager mode, `a * b + c` for fp16 tensors uses `__half` or `__half2` on Ampere/Hopper, unless autocast is on. So fp16 arithmetic is fine. To be safe, we can keep float intermediate math. So the hidden reference likely does: ```python def forward(q, k, cos, sin): # q: [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size] # cos, sin: [num_tokens, rot_dim] - already indexed by position q_embed = (q[..., :rot_dim] * cos.unsqueeze(1)) + (rotate_half_for_neox(q[..., :rot_dim]) * sin.unsqueeze(1)) ... ``` Actually, let me look at a critically important point: the test provides cos and sin of shape [NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM], but in many implementations cos/sin for each token consist of head_size elements, not rot_dim. rot_dim might be the rotation dimension (the part of head_size that gets rotated). In our case, head_size=128 and rot_dim=64. So only the first 64 elements of the 128-element head get rotated. Now, for a Neox-style RoPE where the first 64 elements are rotated: - q[..., :64] gets rotated - q[..., 64:] stays unchanged And the rotation operates on pairs (0, 32), (1, 33), ..., (31, 63). In this case, cos and sin each need to have 32 values per token, but they're often repeated (concatenated with themselves) to get 64 values, so that element-wise multiplication works: ```python cos = torch.cat([cos_32, cos_32], dim=-1) # [num_tokens, 64] sin = torch.cat([sin_32, sin_32], dim=-1) # [num_tokens, 64] ``` Then: ```python q_rot = q[..., :64] q_rotated = q_rot * cos + rotate_half(q_rot) * sin q_out = torch.cat([q_rotated, q[..., 64:]], dim=-1) ``` Where `rotate_half` for Neox style means: ```python def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., :x.shape[-1]//2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1]//2:] return torch.cat([-x2, x1], dim=-1) ``` So for a position in the first half (i < 32): - output[i] = x[i] * cos[i] - x[i + 32] * sin[i] For a position in the second half (i >= 32): - output[i] = x[i] * cos[i] + x[i - 32] * sin[i] Wait, in the `rotate_half` function above: - First half of output is -x2 = -x[..., 32:] - Second half of output is x1 = x[..., :32] So for the first half positions (0 to 31): - rotate_half(q)[i] = -q[..., i + 32] - output[i] = q[..., i] * cos[i] + (-q[..., i + 32]) * sin[i] = q[..., i] * cos[i] - q[..., i + 32] * sin[i] For the second half positions (32 to 63): - rotate_half(q)[i] = q[..., i - 32] - output[i] = q[..., i] * cos[i] + q[..., i - 32] * sin[i] This matches the original code if cos and sin are already concatenated (shape [num_tokens, 64])! In the standard rotary embedding (used by LLaMA, Mistral, etc.): ```python def apply_rotary_emb(x, cos, sin): # x: [..., seq_len, n_heads, head_dim] # cos, sin: [..., seq_len, 1, head_dim] x1 = x[..., ::2] # even indices x2 = x[..., 1::2] # odd indices # cos and sin should be properly shaped x_rot = torch.empty_like(x) x_rot[..., ::2] = x1 * cos - x2 * sin x_rot[..., 1::2] = x1 * sin + x2 * cos return x_rot ``` This is non-NEOX style. The NEOX style (used by GPT-NeoX, CodeLlama): ```python def apply_rotary_emb_neox(x, cos, sin): # x: [..., seq_len, n_heads, head_dim] x1 = x[..., :head_dim//2] x2 = x[..., head_dim//2:] return torch.cat([ x1 * cos - x2 * sin, x1 * sin + x2 * cos ], dim=-1) ``` Most modern models use the non-NEOX style. But wait, some recent models DO use the NEOX style. Which one does the hidden reference use? Actually, let me look at the I/O contract more carefully. The hidden reference takes `q, k, cos, sin` where `cos` and `sin` are shape `[NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM]`. If the hidden reference is using a standard PyTorch implementation, `cos` might need to be `unsqueeze`ed to broadcast correctly. One thing I notice is that my v1 through v5 implementations all use `q.contiguous()` and `k.contiguous()`. The hidden reference might not do this. But that's fine - making inputs contiguous shouldn't change correctness. Another thought: maybe the hidden reference uses `positions` to index into a cache, but the I/O contract provides already-indexed `cos` and `sin`? In that case, the reference might be something like: ```python class ReferenceModel(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): self.rot_dim = rot_dim def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): # q: [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size] # cos: [num_tokens, rot_dim] # But maybe cos contains [cos, sin] concatenated? ``` Hmm wait, in the original code, `cos_sin_cache` is `[max_position, rot_dim]` = `[max_position, 64]`. The cos values are first 32 elements, sin values are next 32 elements. But in the I/O, `cos = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, 64)` and `sin = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, 64)`. That's 64 elements each. But we only need 32. Unless... the I/O contract actually means that `cos` and `sin` have `embed_dim = 32` values but the shape is erroneously written as `[NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM]` because that's the size of the cache? No, `get_inputs` explicitly creates `torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM)` where `ROT_DIM = 64`. Or maybe the hidden reference reshapes `cos` to have `embed_dim` from `ROT_DIM`. Maybe `cos[:, :self.rot_dim//2]` is used. Let me try one more approach. What if the hidden reference uses the NEOX style? Let me create a test kernel that uses NEOX style and see if it matches. Actually, wait. Let me re-read the original kernel code VERY carefully: ```cpp template <typename scalar_t, typename cache_t, bool IS_NEOX> __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( const int64_t* __restrict__ positions, scalar_t* __restrict__ query, scalar_t* __restrict__ key, const cache_t* __restrict__ cos_sin_cache, const int rot_dim, ... ) ``` And the host function `rotary_embedding` in vLLM: - Takes `positions`, `query`, `key`, `head_size`, `cos_sin_cache`, `is_neox`, `rope_dim_offset`, `inverse` But the I/O contract `get_inputs()` gives us `q, k, cos, sin`, not `positions` and `cos_sin_cache`. So the hidden reference must be a wrapper that takes `q, k, cos, sin` and applies rotary embedding. The reference likely does NOT use positions or a cache. A typical PyTorch reference would be: ```python class Model(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): super().__init__() self.rot_dim = rot_dim def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): # q: [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size] # k: [num_tokens, num_kv_heads, head_size] # cos: [num_tokens, rot_dim] or [num_tokens, rot_dim//2] # sin: [num_tokens, rot_dim] or [num_tokens, rot_dim//2] # Reshape q and k to apply rotation on rot_dim # Standard approach: interleaved pairs x1 = q[..., :self.rot_dim][..., ::2] # [num_tokens, num_heads, rot_dim/2] x2 = q[..., :self.rot_dim][..., 1::2] # [num_tokens, num_heads, rot_dim/2] # cos and sin might need reshaping for broadcasting # [num_tokens, rot_dim/2] -> [num_tokens, 1, rot_dim/2] c = cos[..., :self.rot_dim//2].unsqueeze(1) s = sin[..., :self.rot_dim//2].unsqueeze(1) q_rot = q.clone() q_rot[..., :self.rot_dim][..., ::2] = x1 * c - x2 * s q_rot[..., :self.rot_dim][..., 1::2] = x1 * s + x2 * c # Same for k ... return q_rot, k_rot ``` But the shape of `cos` and `sin` is `[NUM_TOKENS, 64]` where `64 = ROT_DIM`. If the reference only uses `cos[:, :32]`, then that's fine. My CUDA kernels access `cos[token * cs_stride_t + pair]` where `pair` goes from 0 to `embed_dim-1 = 31`, which only accesses the first 32 elements. OK let me try another version. What if the hidden reference uses the NEOX style? Let me create a test for that. But first, let me also consider: maybe the I/O contract `cos` and `sin` are already just `[NUM_TOKENS, embed_dim]` shapes. The `get_inputs()` says `torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM)` but when the tensor actually gets created, maybe the reference implementation doesn't rely on the exact shape. Actually, wait - I just realized something. The original kernel was from vLLM, and the `get_inputs()` creates random tensors. If the reference was the vLLM kernel, the inputs would be positions, cos_sin_cache, etc. But the I/O contract gives different input shapes. So the hidden reference is NOT the raw vLLM kernel - it's a different model that wraps the concept. Let me try to infer what the hidden reference does by trying both NEOX and non-NEOX styles. I'll write a simple kernel for each and see which one passes. Actually, I just realized another issue. In my kernels, I use: ```cpp scalar_t* base = q + token * q_stride_t + head * q_stride_h; ``` And then I access `base[...]`. But `q_stride_t` might not equal `num_heads * head_size` if the tensor already has some specific strides. However, since I call `.contiguous()` in the Python wrapper, the strides should be standard. For a contiguous tensor of shape `[num_tokens, num_heads, head_size]`, the strides are `[num_heads * head_size, head_size, 1]`. So `q_stride_t = num_heads * head_size = 32 * 128 = 4096`, and `q_stride_h = head_size = 128`. That means `base` points to the start of a specific head: `q[token, head, :]`. For a contiguous tensor, `base[i]` accesses `q[token, head, i]`. Now, `rope_dim_offset = 0` (from `get_init_inputs()` which returns `[ROT_DIM]`). So we're modifying elements starting from index 0 of each head. And `rot_dim = 64`, so we modify elements 0 to 63. For non-NEOX style: - Pair 0: elements 0, 1 rotated by cos[0], sin[0] - Pair 1: elements 2, 3 rotated by cos[1], sin[1] - ... - Pair 31: elements 62, 63 rotated by cos[31], sin[31] For NEOX style: - Pair 0: elements 0, 32 rotated by cos[0], sin[0] - Pair 1: elements 1, 33 rotated by cos[1], sin[1] - ... - Pair 31: elements 31, 63 rotated by cos[31], sin[31] Given that my non-NEOX v5 had a large error, let me try NEOX:
